[^177]: July 8: Holy Great-Martyr Procopius of Caesarea in Palestine (d. 303 under Emperor Diocletian).
[^178]: Acts 9:3–6. Procopius (originally named Neanias), like Saul on the road to Damascus, was converted by a heavenly vision and a voice calling him by name.
[^179]: In his hagiography, Neanias was granted a miraculous silver cross that secured victory in battle against the Saracens before his trial and open confession of Christ.
[^180]: Acts 9:3–5; 26:14–18.
[^181]: 2 Tim. 4:7. Procopius was tortured in Caesarea by the Roman governor with three-pronged iron scrapers and fire, yet remained steadfast.
[^182]: Procopius' martyrdom inspired the conversion of his mother Theodosia, twelve noble women, and two military tribunes (Nicostratus and Antiochus) with their soldiers, all of whom were martyred together with him.
[^183]: A classic Patristic expression of the doctrine of *theosis* (deification by grace): "by position/adoption a god, through the deification of participation" (Greek: θέσει θεὸς χρηματίσας τῇ μεθέξει τῆς θεώсеως; cf. Ps. 81:6; Jn. 10:34–35; 2 Pet. 1:4).
[^184]: Exod. 14:21–29; 17:11–12. Heirmos of Ode 1, Tone 4.
[^185]: Acts 9:3–6; 26:14–18. Like Saul, Neanias (Procopius) was called by a heavenly voice and vision of Christ.
[^186]: 1 Kg. (1 Sam.) 2:2; 1 Cor. 1:24, 30. Heirmos of Ode 3, Tone 4.
[^187]: Gen. 3:21. The "garments of skins" (*tunicae pelliceae*) symbolize mortal corruptibility assumed after the Fall, stripped away through martyrdom.
[^188]: Hab. 3:11. Heirmos of Ode 4, Tone 4.
[^189]: Isa. 26:9–11. Heirmos of Ode 5, Tone 4.
[^190]: Procopius converted his company of soldiers, led by the tribunes Nicostratus and Antiochus.
[^191]: Twelve Christian women of senatorial rank in Caesarea who confessed Christ and were martyred with Procopius.
[^192]: Jon. 2:10; Jn. 19:34. Heirmos of Ode 6, Tone 4.
[^193]: Procopius' mother, Saint Theodosia of Caesarea, who initially accused her son but was moved to repentance and martyrdom by his steadfastness.
[^194]: Dan. 3:26, 53. Heirmos of Ode 7, Tone 4.
[^195]: Dan. 6:16–23; 3:19–25. Heirmos of Ode 8, Tone 4.
[^196]: Lk. 1:48.
[^197]: Dan. 2:34, 45; Eph. 2:20. Heirmos of Ode 9, Tone 4.
[^198]: In Greek, Procopius (Προκόπιος) derives from προκοπή, meaning "progress, advancement".
[^199]: The two companies of martyrs converted by Procopius: the soldiers led by Antiochus and Nicostratus, and the twelve senatorial women together with his mother Theodosia.
[^200]: Heb. 12:4.
[^201]: Ps. 67:36, 27 (LXX). Prokeimenon and verse of the Common of a Martyr.
[^202]: 2 Tim. 2:1–10.
[^203]: Ps. 91:13 (LXX).
[^204]: Mt. 10:16–22.
[^205]: Ps. 111:6b (LXX).
[^206]: July 9: Holy Hieromartyr Pancratius, Bishop of Taormina (Tauromenium) in Sicily (1st century), disciple of the Apostle Peter.
[^207]: Mt. 16:18. According to tradition, the Apostle Peter consecrated Pancratius as bishop and sent him to evangelize Sicily.
[^208]: Sicily (in the West) was illuminated with the Gospel by Pancratius, becoming spiritually an "East" (Orient).
[^209]: 1 Cor. 13:12 ("For now we see in a mirror, dimly, but then face to face").
[^210]: Mt. 7:14.
[^211]: Exod. 15:1, 6. Heirmos of Ode 1, Tone 4.
[^212]: Isa. 9:2; Mt. 4:16; Lk. 1:79.
[^213]: Gen. 1:6–9; Ps. 135:6 (LXX). Heirmos of Ode 3, Tone 4.
[^214]: The Apostle Peter sent Pancratius to Sicily (the Western Mediterranean).
[^215]: Heb. 9:4.
[^216]: Gen. 28:12.
[^217]: 2 Cor. 5:18–19.
[^218]: Hab. 3:2 (LXX). Heirmos of Ode 4, Tone 4.
[^219]: Exod. 17:6; Num. 20:11; 1 Cor. 10:4.
[^220]: 1 Cor. 1:20.
[^221]: 1 Thess. 5:5.
[^222]: A precise dogmatic formula affirming Christ's two natures (essences) and two wills (*duo themata* / *dyothelitism*), defined by the Sixth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople III, 680–681).
[^223]: Isa. 26:12–13; Rom. 14:9. Heirmos of Ode 5, Tone 4.
[^224]: Judg. 6:37–40; Ps. 71:6 (LXX).
[^225]: Jon. 2:1–3, 10. Heirmos of Ode 6, Tone 4.
[^226]: Rom. 15:16.
[^227]: Rom. 12:1.
[^228]: Dan. 3:23–26, 52–56. Heirmos of Ode 7, Tone 4.
[^229]: Ps. 136:9 (LXX); cf. Mt. 21:44.
[^230]: Dan. 3:57. Heirmos of Ode 8, Tone 4.
[^231]: Note on the icon of Christ painted and carried by St. Pancratius, as recounted in his Life by Evagrius.
[^232]: Heirmos of Ode 9, Tone 4 (cf. Ode 9 of Heirmologion).
[^233]: Basil the Great, *On the Holy Spirit*, 18.45: "For the honor paid to the image passes to the prototype."
[^234]: The Forty-five Martyrs of Nicopolis suffered under Emperor Licinius (c. 319). St. Anthony of the Caves (983–1073), founder of the Kyiv-Caves Lavra, is commemorated on July 10; his full separate festal service follows immediately in this Menaion edition.
[^235]: Isaiah 53:2.
[^236]: The Greek acrostic of the canon by Joseph the Hymnographer: Θεοστεφῆ φάлаγγα μέλπω μαρτύρων ("I sing the God-crowned company of martyrs").
[^237]: Heirmos of Ode 1, Tone 5 (Exodus 15:1–19).
[^238]: Heirmos of Ode 3, Tone 5.
[^239]: Heirmos of Ode 4, Tone 5 (Habakkuk 3:2).
[^240]: Heirmos of Ode 5, Tone 5 (Isaiah 26:9).
[^241]: Heirmos of Ode 6, Tone 5 (Jonah 2:3–7).
[^242]: Kontakion of the Forty-five Martyrs of Nicopolis, Tone 2.
[^243]: Heirmos of Ode 7, Tone 5 (Daniel 3:52).
[^244]: Heirmos of Ode 8, Tone 5.
[^245]: Heirmos of Ode 9, Tone 5.
[^246]: 2 Timothy 2:5 ("if a man also strive for masteries, yet is he not crowned, except he strive lawfully").
[^247]: St. Anthony of the Caves (Antony of Pechersk, 983–1073), founder of the Kyiv-Caves Lavra and father of East Slavic monasticism.
[^248]: Allusion to the call of Abraham (Genesis 12:1–4), who left his homeland at God's call; St. Anthony departed Rus' for Mount Athos, where he was tonsured at Esphigmenou before returning to Kyiv.
[^249]: Standard dismissive Theotokion: "O Mother of God, thou art the vine that bare the true cluster of grapes..."
[^250]: Psalm 115:6 (LXX) / Psalm 116:15.
[^251]: Psalm 111:1 (LXX) / Psalm 112:1.
[^252]: The Dormition Cathedral of the Kyiv-Caves Lavra, founded by St. Anthony in 1073 according to the miraculous measure of the golden belt given by Shimon the Varangian.
[^253]: Troparion / Dismissal Troparion of St. Anthony of Pechersk, Tone 4.
[^254]: Psalm 1:1 et seq.
[^255]: Account from the *Pechersk Paterikon*: St. Anthony foretold the defeat of the Rus' princes at the River Alta (1068) and the survival of the Varangian prince Shimon (Simon) Africanovich, who gifted his gold belt and crown to measure and adorn the Great Church of the Caves.
[^256]: Discourse 16 of the *Kyivan Caves Paterikon*: Monk Erasmus had spent all his wealth on adornments for the monastery church, was subsequently tempted to despair by the devil, fell into grave illness, but was saved and healed through the vision of St. Anthony and St. Theodosius.
[^257]: Tone 6 Dogmatic Theotokion: "Who will not call thee blessed, O all-holy Virgin? Who will not sing thy childbirth without travail?..."
[^258]: Composite monastic readings: (1) Wisdom of Solomon 3:1–9; (2) Wisdom of Solomon 5:15–6:3; (3) Wisdom of Solomon 4:7–15 (as indicated under July 5 for St. Athanasius of Mount Athos).
[^259]: Allusion to St. Anthony the Great of Egypt (c. 251–356), the father of Christian monasticism, whose asceticism and solitary cave dwelling St. Anthony of Kyiv emulated.
[^260]: The Cuman (Polovtsian) invasions of Kyivan Rus', foretold by St. Anthony prior to the Battle of the Alta River.
[^261]: Judges 6:36–40.
[^262]: Narrative of the miraculous founding of the Great Church of the Dormition in the Kyiv-Caves Lavra, where Prince Svyatoslav II Yaroslavich participated in laying out the site revealed by the dew on the fleece.
[^263]: Psalm 115:6 (LXX) / Psalm 116:15.
[^264]: Psalm 91:13 (LXX) / Psalm 92:12 ("The righteous shall flourish like the palm tree").
[^265]: Romans 12:1.
[^266]: Psalm 111:1 (LXX) / Psalm 112:1.
[^267]: Reference to the miracle recounted in the Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon where St. Anthony prayed and fire fell from heaven to burn away the thorns and bushes, purifying the site designated by the Mother of God for the Great Church of the Dormition.
[^268]: Incipit of the Sunday Dogmatic Theotokion in Tone 6: *Творецъ и избавитель мой* (My Creator and Deliverer, Christ the Lord).
[^269]: Troparion of St. Anthony of the Caves in Tone 4, referencing the Small Vespers text: *Ѿ мїрскагѡ мѧтежа изшедъ*. Masoretic Psalm references omitted per LXX standard.
[^270]: LXX Ps. 115:6 [116:15].
[^271]: Matthew 11:27–30 (Conception 43: "All things are delivered unto Me of My Father...").
[^272]: Church Slavonic *любостра́нство* translates Greek *xeniteia* (voluntary exile, pilgrimage, living as a stranger for Christ). Anthony departed from Liubech to Mount Athos.
[^273]: Refers to the Athos abbot who tonsured Anthony on the Holy Mountain and prophetically commanded him to return to Rus'.
[^274]: Reference to the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra and the Great Church of the Dormition, dedicated to the Theotokos.
[^275]: 1 Kings 2:2 [1 Samuel 2:2]. Masoretic versification bracketed per MTS-1.
[^276]: Slavonic *въ рѡссі́и* ("in Rus'"). Refers to the prophecy of the Athonite abbot recorded in the Life of St. Anthony in the Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon.
[^277]: Reference to St. Anthony's courageous rebukes to Grand Prince Iziaslav of Kyiv and other potentates in defense of the oppressed, as recorded in the Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon.
[^278]: Reference to St. Anthony's reclusion in the underground cave of Berestovo (Kyiv Caves).
[^279]: Allusion to John 14:23.
[^280]: LXX Ps. 131:4–5 [132:4–5].
[^281]: The Kontakion celebrates Anthony's forty-year ascetic reclusion in the Kyiv caves and his celestial intercession.
[^282]: The Ikos documents the forty-year ascetic reclusion (*четыредесѧтолѣ́тное вре́мѧ*) of St. Anthony in the Near Caves (Berestovo/Pechersk) without emerging.
[^283]: Metropolitan Hilarion of Kyiv (referred to epigraphically here as *А҆рхїепⷭ҇кпа і҆ларі́ѡна*), author of the *Sermon on Law and Grace*, dug the initial cave on the Berestovo hill before being consecrated Metropolitan of Kyiv in 1051. Anthony subsequently inhabited and expanded it.
[^284]: St. Theodosius of the Caves (†1074), Anthony's co-founder and organizer of cenobitic monasticism at the Kyiv Caves.
[^285]: Refers to St. Anthony's journey from Liubech to Mount Athos and his blessed return with the blessing of the Athonite Hegumen.
[^286]: Allusion to the parting testament of St. Anthony to his disciples in the Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon, promising to remain with them in spirit and protect the Lavra.
[^287]: St. Anthony the Great of Egypt (c. 251–356), the father of Christian monasticism, after whom St. Anthony of the Caves was named in tonsure.
[^288]: St. Anthony is venerated as the *первонача́лникъ* (first-founder / proto-archimandrite) of cave-dwelling monasticism in Rus'.
[^289]: Recapitulation of the miracle of the celestial fire consuming the shrubs on the site of the Pechersk Dormition Cathedral.
[^290]: St. Pachomius the Great (c. 292–348), founder of Christian cenobitic monasticism in Egypt, who received the monastic rule from an angel.
[^291]: Refers to the foundation of the Great Stone Cathedral of the Dormition of the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra, founded by St. Anthony and St. Theodosius in 1073.
[^292]: Mt. 25:21, 23.
[^293]: Incipit of the Marian Theotokion: *Accept, O Sovereign Lady, the prayers of thy servants, and deliver us from every distress and affliction.*
[^294]: Ps. 115:6 (LXX) / Ps. 116:15 (MT). Verse: Ps. 115:3 (LXX) / Ps. 116:12 (MT). Epistle: Galatians, Pericope 213 [Gal. 5:22–6:2]. Alleluia: Ps. 111:1 (LXX) / Ps. 112:1 (MT). Gospel: Matthew, Pericope 10 [Mt. 4:25–5:12]. Communion Verse: Ps. 111:6b (LXX) / Ps. 112:6b (MT).
[^295]: Commemoration of the miracle of the Holy Great-Martyr Euphemia the All-Praised at the Council of Chalcedon (451 AD), whereby the Orthodox Tome of Leo and definition of faith was confirmed and the Monophysite tome rejected.
[^296]: Song of Songs 1:3–4; 2:5.
[^297]: Lk. 2:35.
[^298]: Ps. 67:36 (LXX) / Ps. 68:35 (MT).
[^299]: Rom. 6:4; Col. 2:12.
[^300]: Ex. 14:16, 21–28.
[^301]: The classic Ode 3 Heirmos attributed to St. John Damascene.
[^302]: Allusion to the miraculous flowing of fragrant blood and healing fluid from the relics of the Great-Martyr Euphemia.
[^303]: Hab. 3:2 (LXX).
[^304]: Is. 26:9.
[^305]: Jon. 2:3–8.
[^306]: The historical petition invoking the Fourth Ecumenical Council (Chalcedon, 451 AD), which gathered 630 Fathers whose Tome and definition of faith were miraculously preserved by the Great-Martyr Euphemia.
[^307]: Dan. 3:19–26.
[^308]: Dan. 3:19, 52–90.
[^309]: Hab. 3:2 and the mystery of the Incarnation.
[^310]: Ps. 109:3 (LXX) / Ps. 110:3 (MT).
[^311]: Ex. 12:7, 13; Eph. 4:30.
[^312]: Ps. 67:27 (LXX) / Ps. 68:26 (MT).
[^313]: 2 Corinthians, Pericope 181 [2 Cor. 6:1–10]. Alleluia: Ps. 39:2 (LXX) / Ps. 40:1 (MT). Gospel: Luke, Pericope 33 [Lk. 7:36–50]. Communion Verse: Ps. 111:6b (LXX) / Ps. 112:6b (MT).
[^314]: Slavonic: *Мц҃а тогѡ́же, въ в҃і де́нь. Ст҃ы́хъ мч҃нкъ Про́кла и҆ Їла́рїа: И҆ прпⷣбнагѡ ѻ҆ц҃а̀ на́шегѡ Мїхаи́ла малеї́на* (July 12). Proclus and Hilarion of Ancyra suffered under Trajan (early 2nd c.); St. Michael Maleinos (c. 894–961) was a Byzantine monastic founder on Mount Kyminas in Bithynia and spiritual father to St. Athanasius of Athos.
[^315]: Slavonic: *и҆ ꙗ҆́кѡ сѣ́на сла́вы вре́менныѧ, не све́рпа, ше́велѧ є҆сѝ, сла́вы бж҃твенныѧ не забꙋ́дꙋ* (Fol. 55r col. 1–2). An unusual Ruthenian print reading representing Greek: καὶ ὡς χόρτον δόξης προσκαίρου οὐκ ἐφείσω (and as grass didst not spare passing glory); the Pochaiv print exhibits corrupt orthography combining *не сверпа* with *шевеля* and first-person *не забуду*.
[^316]: Rubric: *И҆ ны́нѣ̀, бг҃о, и҆лѝ крⷭтобг҃о* (Fol. 55v col. 1). When the feast falls on a Wednesday or Friday, the Stavrotheotokion is chanted instead of the standard Theotokion.
[^317]: Rubric: *И҆ ны́нѣ̀, бг҃о: и҆лѝ крⷭтобг҃о.* Text incipit: *Ю҆́ница нескве́рнаѧ...* (The undefiled Heifer...). A classic Byzantine Stavrotheotokion based on Jeremiah 50:11 and Patristic typology.
[^318]: Slavonic: *бл҃гоче́* (Fol. 56r col. 1), scribal/typesetter abbreviation in the Pochaiv print for *благоче́стная* (or *благодатная*), Greek: κεχαριτωμένη.
[^319]: Slavonic: *по́ѧсъ ѿве́ргъ* (Fol. 56v col. 2). In Byzantine administrative practice, the military and patrician belt (*cingulum*, ζώνη) was the official insignia of senatorial or high court office; Michael Maleinos was nephew to Emperor Romanos I Lekapenos and of high patrician rank before renouncing the court for the monastic habit.
[^320]: Slavonic: *Подо́бенъ: Премꙋ́дрости* (Fol. 57r col. 1). Canonical melodic model: *Tone 8, Podoben: "The Wisdom and Word" (Премꙋ́дрости и҆ Сло́ва)*.
[^321]: Slavonic: *пло́ти не покрыва́ѧ бро́ви* (Fol. 58r col. 1), literally "not covering the brows/eyelids of the flesh," an idiom for keeping unbroken monastic vigil without sleep.
[^322]: Slavonic: *Зако́нѡмъ хва́льнымъ не порабо́тѧтъ* (Fol. 58r col. 2), representing Greek: ἐπαίνων νόμοις οὐχ ὑποπίπτει (the martyrs' courage falls not under / cannot be bound by the conventional rules of laudation).
[^323]: Slavonic: *Малос҃ло́вїе лѣ́ностное ᲂу҆ма̀ ѿгна́въ* (Fol. 58r col. 2). In ascetic literature, *малословие* here refers to mental sluggishness or brevity in contemplation.
[^324]: Slavonic: *Подо́бенъ: Тве́рдыѧ* (Fol. 58v col. 1). Full canonical incipit: *Tone 2, Podoben: "Seeking the highest / The firm and divinely-speaking preachers" (Тве́рдыѧ и҆ бг҃овѣща̑нныѧ проповѣ́датели)*.
[^325]: Slavonic: *водрꙋзи́въ є҆диноѡбра́знѡ сѣ́ни ꙗ҆́кѡ песо́къ* (Fol. 59v col. 1). *Сѣ́ни* (literally tabernacles, booths, cells) refers to the monastic lavriotic dwellings or sketes founded by St. Michael Maleinos on Mount Kyminas.
[^326]: The Exapostilarion melodic model *Небо звездами* ("The heaven with stars") is traditionally assigned to Tone 3.
[^327]: The Synaxis of the Holy Archangel Gabriel is celebrated on July 13 (as well as on March 26, the day following the Annunciation). Commemorated with him is our Venerable Father Stephen the Sabaite (725–794/807), hymnographer and monk of the Great Lavra of Saint Sabas.
[^328]: Church Slavonic *къ первоенно́вномꙋ* represents a typographer's metathesis for *первовино́вномꙋ* (unto the First Cause / Author of all).
[^329]: Church Slavonic *мольбꙋ̀ ѿтря́съ*: the Slavonic *мольба* translates Greek τύρβη / θόρυβος (worldly turmoil, cares, or anxieties), hence "shaking off worldly turmoil".
[^330]: The Greek acrostic reads Ὡς παμμέγιστον Γαβριὴλ μέλπω τάχα ("I sing of Gabriel, the truly greatest"). The Slavonic text reflects this as *Ꙗ҆́кѡ всечⷭтна гаврїи́ла хвалю̀* ("As all-honorable I praise Gabriel").
[^331]: The phrase "activity... and vision" translates the classical ascetical dichotomy of *πρᾶξις* (ascetical labor) and *θεωρία* (spiritual contemplation / divine vision).
[^332]: The Kontakion closes with a liturgical adaptation of Romans 8:31: *са́мъ бꙋ́ди помо́щникъ, и҆ никто́же на ны̀* ("Be thou our helper, and none shall be against us!").
[^333]: Church Slavonic *свѣтонаро́чникꙋ* represents a typographical slip for *свѣтонача́льникꙋ* (Author / Source of Light, Greek φῶς ἀρχηγόν).
[^334]: Scriptural allusion to Luke 15:7, 10: "joy shall be in heaven over one sinner that repents".
[^335]: A hymnographic wordplay upon the name *Stephen* (Greek Στέφανος, *Stephanos*, meaning "crown" or "wreath"): "Christ the Life-Giver hath placed a goodly crown upon thy head, O namesake Stephen (*тезоимени́те стефа́не*)".
[^336]: Church Slavonic *Королю́ же да́рꙋй побѣди́тєлнаѧ* ("And unto the King grant victories"): The Pochaiv Lavra edition explicitly preserves the supplication for the Polish-Lithuanian monarch (*rex* / *король*), reflecting the historical civic and ecclesiastical context of Western Ruthenia prior to the partitions of Poland.
[^337]: *From earliest infancy* translates the Greek/Slavonic idiom *ѿ мла́дыхъ ногте́й* (literally *from tender fingernails*, cf. Greek *ἐξ ἁπαλῶν ὀνύχων*), denoting from one's earliest childhood or infancy.
[^338]: *Exactors* (*и҆стѧза́телей*, Greek *τελωνῶν* / *λογοθετῶν*): the demonic inquisitors or toll-house tax-collectors encountered at the soul's departure from the body.
[^339]: *Lucifer* translates *денни́ца* (the morning star / day-star, Latin *Lucifer*, Isa. 14:12), referring to the fallen archangel Satan.
[^340]: *Childish-minded* translates *младоꙋ́мнаго* (Greek *νηπιόφρονος*), contrasting with the three-year-old martyr Cyricus who possessed a "mature mind" (*соверше́ннымъ ᲂу҆мо́мъ*).
[^341]: *By paradoxical deeds* (*Преквїрными*): an East Slavic/Ruthenian liturgical calque (cf. Polish *przekorny* / Greek *παραδόξοις*) indicating paradoxical, contrary-to-nature wonders, whereby mortal suffering is exchanged for immutable heavenly glory.
[^342]: *Threefold-named* translates *Трезоимени́тѡ* (or *тезоимени́тѡ* / Greek *ὁμωνύμως* or *τρισεπώνυме*); Cyricus (Greek *Κύρικος*, from *Κύριος*, Lord) is hailed as named after the Lord.
[^343]: *Guide of strangers* (*странноводе́цъ* / *страннокоде́цъ*): referring to the holy Apostle Aquila's hospitality and accompaniment of the Apostle Paul as recorded in the Book of Acts (Acts 18:2–3, 18, 26).
[^344]: *Named after the Lord* (*Господоиме́ннаѧ*, Greek *Κυριώνυμος*): an epithet of the Theotokos as Lady (*Δέσποινα* / *Domina*), corresponding to the Lord (*Κύριος* / *Dominus*).
[^345]: *A young heifer, a sacredly-fair young maiden* (*тели́ца ю́на ю҆ни́ца сщ҃еннопрекра́сна*): poetically contrasting the mother (the young heifer / *δάμαλις*) bearing her offspring to the sacrificial slaughter, offering him to Christ the Lamb.
[^346]: *Bearing the Light that shone forth from Light*: alluding to the vision of Zechariah (Zech. 4:2) and the Nicene Creed (*Light of Light*), applied to the Mother of God bearing the pre-eternal Word.
[^347]: *Sweet-briar* translates *шипо́къ* (wild rose / sweet-briar / eglantine, Greek *ῥόδον* / *κυνόροδον*), paired with *крї́нъ* (lily) as imagery of martyric purity and sweet aroma.
[^348]: *Co-unoriginate, without division in hypostases* (*собезнача́льнѣ... неразлѣ́чнѡ въ ᲂу҆поста́сѣхъ*): precise Trinitarian and Christological terminology confessing the Son as co-eternal with the Father, distinct in hypostasis yet undivided in divine essence.
[^349]: *The author of evil* translates *безначя́льнаго врага̀*, literally *the enemy from the beginning* or *the arch-enemy* (Greek *τὸν ἀρχέκακον ἐχθρόν*); Church Slavonic *безначальный* here reflects *ἀρχέκακος* rather than unoriginate.
[^350]: *A mature understanding* (*сѣ́дъ ра́зꙋмъ*, literally *gray-haired intellect*, Greek *πολιὰν φρόνησιν*): a classical patristic trope describing youthful martyrs who possessed the wisdom of venerable old age.
[^351]: *Born of the Varangians* (*ѿ варѧ̑гъ роди́сѧ*): referring to the Scandinavian/Norse (Varangian, Rurikid) lineage of Grand Prince Volodymyr the Great.
[^352]: *Overshadowed by the Mosaic law* translates *мѡѷсе́йскимъ ѡ҆тне́вшасѧ [ѡ҆тѣне́на бы́вши] зако́номъ*, referring to the giving of the Law on Mount Sinai amidst clouds and thick darkness (Ex. 19:16–18), contrasted here with the bright light of Christian illumination in Kyiv.
[^353]: *A father spiritually and a ruler physically* (*ѻ҆ц҃ъ дх҃о́внѡ, цр҃ь же чꙋ́вственнѡ*): contrasting the spiritual fatherhood of the baptizer with the temporal, sensory (*чꙋ́вственнѡ*, Greek *αἰσθητῶς*) rule of the monarch.
[^354]: *With his foremother Helen*: Princess Olha of Kyiv, who took the Christian name Helen (*Олена* / *Елена*) at her baptism in Constantinople.
[^355]: *Roman and David*: the baptismal Christian names of the Holy Passion-bearers Boris and Gleb.
[^356]: *Unto our king victories over heathen enemies* (*коро́лю же на́шемꙋ пода́ти побѣ̑ды на пога́ныѧ врагѝ*): preserving the diplomatic Ruthenian ink witness of the 1761 Pochaiv Menaion printed in the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth under the reign of King Augustus III of Poland, contrasting with Russian Synodal editions which read *царю нашему* or *императору*.
[^357]: *The wild branches cut off* (*вѣ́тви сѣкꙋ́щыѧ вседи́чныѧ*): alluding to Rom. 11:17–24, the cutting down and grafting of wild olive branches, signifying the pruning away of pagan idolatry.
[^358]: *Unto our king... and victories over the heathen*: repeating the official royal petition for the reigning Polish monarch in the Pochaiv edition.
[^359]: *The Christ-loving king, and the multitude of his subjects* (*хрⷭ҇толюби́вомꙋ королю̀, и҆ мно́жествꙋ владо́мыхъ*): the Pochaiv Troparion concluding petition adapted for the Polish-Lithuanian Crown and its subjects.
[^360]: *Praising thee as his forefather*: referring to the traditional claim of the Kings of Poland and Grand Dukes of Lithuania of dynastic descent or titular inheritance connecting to the ancient Kyivan Rurikid grand princes.
[^361]: *Cast off the disease of the eyes*: alluding to the Kyivan Primary Chronicle account of Prince Volodymyr’s miraculous recovery from sudden blindness upon entering the baptismal font in Chersonesus (Korsun), mirroring the Apostle Paul’s restoration of sight at his baptism by Ananias (Acts 9:18).
[^362]: *Tying him to a horse's tail*: recording the physical destruction of the chief pagan idol Perun in Kyiv, who was dragged at horse-tail down the Borychiv descent to the Dnipro River, as recorded in the Primary Chronicle.
[^363]: *Raised up a divine church in her name*: referring to the famous Church of the Tithes (*Десятинна церква*) in Kyiv, dedicated to the Dormition of the Mother of God, built by Grand Prince Volodymyr.
[^364]: Slavonic: *корсꙋ́на доше́дъ* (having arrived in Cherson/Korsun). The printed text reflects the Primary Chronicle tradition wherein Grand Prince Vladimir was afflicted with blindness prior to his baptism in Cherson and was miraculously healed upon emerging from the font.
[^365]: Refers to Holy Princess Olga (baptized Helen, feast July 11), grandmother of St. Vladimir, who received baptism in Constantinople.
[^366]: Slavonic: *рѡссі́йскїй цр҃ю васі́лїе* (Rus' king/tsar Basil). In East Slavic and Byzantine liturgical panegyric, *tsar* (caesar/basileus) was frequently applied to sovereign Christian monarchs, and Basil was Vladimir's baptismal name, received from his sponsor, Byzantine Emperor Basil II.
[^367]: Isaiah 2:2 / Micah 4:1 (LXX): "In the last days the mountain of the Lord shall be manifest, and the house upon the tops of the mountains."
[^368]: Refers to the Church of the Tithes (Desyatynna) erected by St. Vladimir on the Starokyivska Hill in Kyiv, consecrated in honor of the Dormition of the Mother of God in 996.
[^369]: Allusion to the messianic prophecy of Isaiah 7:14.
[^370]: Slavonic: *хрⷭ҇толюби́вагѡ королѧ̀* (the Christ-loving king). The Pochaiv 1761 edition, printed by the Basilian Fathers within the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth during the reign of Augustus III, retains the Ruthenian Greek Catholic petition for the King of Poland.
[^371]: Slavonic: *въ ко́рсꙋнѣ* (in Cherson/Korsun). The text explicitly names the Crimean city where Prince Vladimir was instructed and baptized.
[^372]: Psalm 111:2 (LXX): "The generation of the upright shall be blessed."
[^373]: Refers to Vladimir's sons, the holy martyrs Boris and Gleb (baptized Roman and David).
[^374]: Refers to Kyiv as the "Mother of Rus' cities" (*Мати градомъ русьскимъ*, Primary Chronicle s.a. 882) and the Church of the Tithes (*Desyatynna*), consecrated to the Dormition of the Theotokos, where the relics of St. Vladimir and St. Olga were translated and enshrined.
[^375]: Psalm 67:16 (LXX): "The mountain of God is a fertile mountain, a curdled mountain."
[^376]: The Church of the Tithes (*Desyatynna*) in Kyiv, founded by St. Vladimir in 989 and consecrated in 996.
[^377]: Roman and David are the Christian baptismal names of the holy passion-bearers Boris and Gleb.
[^378]: Luke 2:35 ("A sword shall pierce through thine own soul also").
[^379]: Refers to the hagiographical tradition of the Hieromartyr Athenogenes, bishop of Heracleopolis (or Sebaste in Armenia), whose hermitage was frequented by wild beasts, notably a hind which brought her fawn to receive the saint's blessing before his martyrdom under Diocletian.
[^380]: Rubrical direction governing the movable Sunday of the Holy Fathers of the Six Ecumenical Councils, which is observed on the Sunday falling between July 13 and July 19.
[^381]: Slavonic: *Подо́бенъ: Ѿча́ѧнныѧ:*. The printed text provides the South-West Ruthenian incipit *Ѿча́ѧнныѧ* corresponding to the standard Byzantine Tone 6 automelon *Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι* (*Всѐ ѿло́жше* / "Having laid aside all earthly hope").
[^382]: An allusion to Genesis 9:23, where Shem and Japheth took a garment and went backward to cover the nakedness of their father Noah without looking upon it.
[^383]: Greek: Ἄρειος ὁ ἐπώνυμος τῆς μανίας ("Arius whose name is synonymous with madness/wrath", playing upon the etymology connecting Arius with Ares and fury).
[^384]: Genesis 14:14–20 (LXX). The 318 servants of Abram are the traditional Scriptural type prefiguring the 318 Holy Fathers who assembled at the First Ecumenical Council in Nicaea (325 AD).
[^385]: Deuteronomy 1:8–11 (LXX).
[^386]: Deuteronomy 10:14–21 (LXX).
[^387]: Severus of Antioch (c. 465–538), leader of the non-Chalcedonians, termed "headless" (Greek: ἀκέφαλος / Slavonic: *безгла́вный*) in reference to the Acephali.
[^388]: The Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed (*въ и҆з̾ѡбраже́нїи вѣ́ры* / *Symbolum Fidei*).
[^389]: St. Germanus I, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–733 or 740), defender of Orthodoxy against Iconoclasm.
[^390]: Reference to the Second Ecumenical Council (First Council of Constantinople, 381 AD), which condemned the Pneumatomachians (Macedonians).
[^391]: St. Cyril of Alexandria, who presided over the Third Ecumenical Council (Ephesus, 431 AD), which condemned Nestorius and affirmed the title *Theotokos*.
[^392]: The 630 Holy Fathers of the Fourth Ecumenical Council (Chalcedon, 451 AD), which condemned Monophysitism (Eutyches and Dioscorus).
[^393]: Slavonic *Ѡ҆ри́ндита* (Orindites), referring to a Monophysite or Severian faction, along with Jacob Baradaeus (*і҆а́кѡвъ*), Theodosius of Alexandria, and Dioscorus.
[^394]: Isaiah 11:2 (LXX), the seven gifts or spirits of the Lord resting upon the Branch of Jesse.
[^395]: St. Cyril of Alexandria.
[^396]: Psalm 79:9 LXX ("Thou hast brought a vine out of Egypt: Thou hast cast out the heathen, and planted it").
[^397]: The Tome of Pope St. Leo the Great to Flavian of Constantinople (*Epistola Dogmatica ad Flavianum*), accepted at the Council of Chalcedon (451 AD) with the acclamation: "Peter has spoken through Leo!"
[^398]: Psalm 49:1 LXX ("The God of gods, even the Lord, hath spoken, and called the earth from the rising of the sun unto the going down thereof").
[^399]: Proverbs 9:1 ("Wisdom hath builded her house, she hath hewn out her seven pillars").
[^400]: The dogmatic epistles of St. Cyril of Alexandria, particularly his letters to John of Antioch and his synodical letters with the Twelve Anathemas against Nestorius.
[^401]: Allusion to Proverbs 9:1–5 and John 7:37 ("If any man thirst, let him come unto Me, and drink").
[^402]: Isaiah 41:4 and 44:6 LXX ("I am God the First, and I am after these things; beside Me there is no God").
[^403]: Bel and the Dragon (Dan. 14:23–27 LXX), where the Prophet Daniel slew the great dragon worshipped by the Babylonians.
[^404]: Reference to the violent death of Arius in Constantinople (336 AD), whose bowels burst asunder in a public latrine, compared to Judas (Acts 1:18).
[^405]: Slavonic *лекїада́мъ* / Greek: ληκυθιστής / ληκυθιάζω, referring to empty flasks, blusterers, or babblers of vanity.
[^406]: The classic formulation of St. Basil the Great on icon veneration (*De Spiritu Sancto*, ch. 18): "The honor paid to the image passes to the prototype" (ἡ γὰρ τῆς εἰκόνος τιμὴ ἐπὶ τὸ πρωτότυπον διαβαίνει), dogmatized by the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787 AD).
[^407]: Reference to 1 Kings 18:38 LXX (3 Kings 18:38), where the Prophet Elijah drenched the sacrificial wood with water, and the divine fire consumed the burnt offering, the wood, the stones, and the water in the trench.
[^408]: Slavonic: *ѻ҆ц҃ъ є҆диновино́венъ є҆́сть*. Greek: μόνος αἴτιος / μοναρχία. The classical patristic doctrine of the monarchy of the Father (*monarchia Patris*) as the sole unoriginate source and cause (αἰτία) of the Son and the Holy Spirit within the Holy Trinity.
[^409]: Slavonic: *семочисле́нныхъ*. The sevenfold-numbered Holy Fathers of the Ecumenical Councils (prefiguring the complete conciliar pleroma of the Church).
[^410]: Slavonic text reads *дбо*, rendering Greek διπλοῦς ("twofold"), an epigraphic contraction or printing error in the Pochaiv edition for *дво́й* / *двоѧ́къ* / *ѻ҆́бо*.
[^411]: Slavonic: *і҆а́кѡва и҆мꙋ́щїи кенто́на назва́нїе*. Transliterating Greek ὁ κέντων (Latin *cento*, a patched garment or rags), the historical epithet of Jacob Baradaeus (c. 500–578), from whom the Syrian non-Chalcedonians were named Jacobites.
[^412]: The Fourth Ecumenical Council (Chalcedon, 451 AD), which condemned Monophysitism and confirmed the dogmatic definition of Christ in two natures, unconfused, unchangeable, indivisible, and inseparable.
[^413]: An allusion to Canon 7 of the Third Ecumenical Council (Ephesus, 431 AD) and the definition of Chalcedon (451 AD), which strictly forbade the composition or alteration of the Symbol of Faith under penalty of anathema.
[^414]: Severus of Antioch (c. 465–538), leader of the Severian Monophysites, condemned by the Synod of Constantinople under Patriarch Mennas in 536 AD.
[^415]: Tone 6 automelon *Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι* (*Всѐ ѿло́жше* / "Having laid aside all earthly hope").
[^416]: St. George, Metropolitan of Nicomedia (9th century), celebrated Byzantine hymnographer and contemporary of St. Photius the Great.
[^417]: The Catechesis of St. Theodore the Studite (*Catecheses Magnae* / *Parvae*), prescribed in traditional monastic typika to be read in the narthex (*litē*) after the Great Doxology on Sundays.
[^418]: The prescribed liturgical pericopes for the Holy Fathers of the Ecumenical Councils: Epistle Hebrews 13:7–16 (Pericope 334), Gospel John 17:1–13 (Pericope 56, Christ's High-Priestly Prayer).
[^419]: The Holy Great-Martyr Marina (Margaret) of Antioch in Pisidia (commemorated July 17), martyred under the Roman prefect Olybrius during the persecution of Diocletian (c. 304 AD).
[^420]: Tone 8 automelon *Ὢ τοῦ παραδόξου θαύματος* (*Ѡ҆ пресла́внагѡ чꙋдесѐ* / "O most marvelous wonder").
[^421]: The Greek and Slavonic alphabetical acrostic: *Χριστοῦ δάμαλιν μάρτυρα μέλπω* / Slavonic: *Хрⷭто́вꙋ дв҃ꙋ мч҃нцꙋ невѣ́стꙋ пѣ́снми пою̀* ("Christ's virgin martyr and bride with songs do I sing").
[^422]: Tone 4 automelon *Ταχὺ προκατάλαβε* (*Ско́рѡ предварѝ* / "Speedily anticipate").
[^423]: Allusion to the traditional hagiographical account of the martyrdom of St. Marina (Margaret), wherein the devil appeared in her prison cell in the form of a monstrous dragon to terrify her; she made the sign of the Cross, seized the beast, and trampled it underfoot.
[^424]: Tone 3 automelon by St. Romanos the Melodist: *Ἡ Παρθένος σήμερον* (*Дѣ́ва дне́сь* / "Today the Virgin").
[^425]: Genesis 49:10 LXX: "A prince shall not fail from Judah, nor a ruler from his loins, until the things come that are stored up for him; and he is the expectation of the nations."
[^426]: Daniel 3:1–7 LXX, describing King Nebuchadnezzar's golden image set up in the plain of Dura, and the refusal of the Three Holy Children (Ananias, Azarias, and Misael) to worship it.
[^427]: An allusion to Genesis 3:24 LXX, where the cherubim and the turning flame-bearing sword (τὴν στρεφομένην ῥομφαίαν / *ѡ҆браща́ющеесѧ ѻ҆рꙋ́жїе*) were stationed to guard the way to the Tree of Life in Eden.
[^428]: Prokeimenon of the Holy Great-Martyr Marina: Psalm 67:36, 27 LXX (68:35, 26).
[^429]: The Epistle reading for a holy martyr or virgin-martyr: 2 Corinthians 6:1–10 (Pericope 181).
[^430]: The Gospel reading for a holy woman or martyr: Luke 7:36–50 (Pericope 33, Christ in the house of Simon the Pharisee).
[^431]: The Holy Martyr Hyacinth of Amastris (in Paphlagonia, martyred under Trajan, c. 98–117 AD) and the Holy Martyr Emilian of Silistria (Durostorum in Moesia Inferior, martyred under Julian the Apostate in 362 AD).
[^432]: Tone 4 automelon *Ὡς γενναῖον ἐν μάρτυσιν* (*Ꙗ҆́кw до́блѧ* / "As one valiant").
[^433]: Acrostic in Church Slavonic: *Є҆міліа́на мч҃нка пою̀ сла́внѡ* ("Emilian the martyr do I sing gloriously"). Greek: *Αἰμιλιανὸν τὸν μάτυρα μέλπω*, composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer.
[^434]: Tone 8 automelon *Τὴν Σοφίαν καὶ Λόγον* (*Премꙋ́дрости* / "Of Wisdom").
[^435]: Tone 3 automelon *Θείας πίστεως* (*Бж҃е́ственныѧ вѣ́ры* / "Of the divine Faith").
[^436]: July 19 commemoration: St. Macrina the Younger (c. 327–379 AD), sister of St. Basil the Great and St. Gregory of Nyssa; and our Venerable Father Dios the Wonderworker (c. 431 AD), archimandrite and founder of the Monastery of St. Dios in Constantinople.
[^437]: Tone 4 automelon *Ἔδωκας σημείωσιν* (*Да́лъ є҆сѝ зна́менїе* / "Thou hast given a sign").
[^438]: Typological Scriptural allusions to the wonders worked by St. Dios: Aaron's dry rod budding (Numbers 17:1–10), Moses bringing water from the dry rock (Exodus 17:1–7; Numbers 20:1–11), and Elijah raising the son of the widow of Zarephath (1 Kings 17:17–24 LXX / 3 Kings 17:17–24).
[^439]: The alphabetic acrostic of the Canon to St. Macrina: *Νύμφην καθαράν σε Χριστῷ Μακρῖνα στέφω* / Slavonic: *Невѣ́стꙋ чи́стꙋ тѧ̀ хрⷭ҇тꙋ̀ макрі́но вѣнча́ю* ("As a pure bride unto Christ do I crown thee, O Macrina").
[^440]: Tone 2 automelon *Τοὺς ἀσφαλεῖς* (*Тве́рдыѧ* / "The firm [and God-proclaimed preachers]").
[^441]: St. Thecla the Protomartyr, disciple of the Apostle Paul. According to St. Gregory of Nyssa's *Life of St. Macrina*, their mother Emmelia had a vision during labor in which an angel directed that the child be named Thecla, though she was publicly called Macrina after her paternal grandmother.
[^442]: "Цр҃кѡмꙋ гра́дꙋ" — Constantine's imperial city, Constantinople (*Царгород*), where St. Dios founded his monastery.
[^443]: "Egypt" is used here allegorically in the ascetic tradition to signify the realm of worldly passions and bondage to the flesh.
[^444]: The holy family of St. Macrina: her grandmother St. Macrina the Elder, her mother St. Emmelia, and her brothers St. Basil the Great, St. Gregory of Nyssa, and St. Peter of Sebaste.
[^445]: Allusion to Psalm 67:14 LXX (68:13): "the wings of a dove covered with silver, and her pinions with the greenness of gold."
[^446]: July 20 commemoration: The Holy and Glorious Prophet Elijah (the Thesbite), one of the greatest prophets of the Old Testament (9th century BC, 3–4 Kings / 1–2 Kings).
[^447]: Rubrical note on the order of service for the Prophet Elijah: explaining that in standard printed and manuscript Greek and Slavonic Typika, Elijah is ranked as a sixfold commemoration (*шестеричная служба*) combined with the weekday Oktoechos, though in festal editions it is frequently served as a Polyeleos or Vigil.
[^448]: Prokeimenon at the Liturgy: Psalm 109:4 LXX (110:4).
[^449]: Prokeimenon verse: Psalm 109:1 LXX (110:1).
[^450]: Epistle pericope: James 5:10–20 (Pericope 57), commending the patience of the prophets and holding up Elijah's fervent prayer that closed and opened the heavens.
[^451]: Alleluia verse: Psalm 98:6 LXX (99:6).
[^452]: Gospel pericope: Luke 4:22–30 (Pericope 14), Christ's sermon in Nazareth recounting Elijah sent to the widow of Zarephath in Sidon and Elisha cleansing Naaman the Syrian.
[^453]: Communion verse (*Kinonikon*): Psalm 111:6b LXX (112:6b).
[^454]: Allusion to the prophetic judgment against Ahab for the judicial murder of Naboth the Jezreelite: 3 Kings 20:19 LXX / 21:19 Masoretic.
[^455]: Aposticha verse: Psalm 98:6 LXX (99:6).
[^456]: Aposticha verse: Psalm 109:4 LXX (110:4).
[^457]: In Church Slavonic *Въ тро́стѣ и҆ ви́хрѣ то́нцѣ*, translating Greek *Ἐν λεπτῇ αὔρᾳ* / *Ἐν λεπτοτάτῃ αὔρᾳ*, the "still small voice" or gentle breeze of 3 Kings 19:12 LXX.
[^458]: The hymnographer Byzantios / Byzantius, a Byzantine composer of the 8th–9th centuries.
[^459]: The first Paremia at Great Vespers: 3 Kings 17:1–24 LXX (1 Kings 17:1–24 Masoretic), recounting Elijah shutting the heavens, being fed by ravens at the brook Cherith, multiplying the meal and oil of the widow of Zarephath, and raising her son from the dead.
[^460]: The second Paremia at Great Vespers: 3 Kings 18:1, 17–46 LXX (1 Kings 18:1, 17–46 Masoretic), recounting Elijah's confrontation with Ahab and the prophets of Baal on Mount Carmel, the descent of fire from heaven, the slaughter of the false prophets at the brook Kishon, and the coming of rain after the prolonged drought.
[^461]: The Slavonic text reads "триста и пятьдесятъ" (three hundred and fifty) prophets of Baal; standard Septuagint and Masoretic witnesses record 450 prophets of Baal alongside 400 prophets of the groves (Asherah).
[^462]: The third Paremia at Great Vespers: 4 Kings 2:1–14 LXX (2 Kings 2:1–14 Masoretic), the translation of Elijah into heaven in a chariot of fire and Elisha receiving his mantle and a double portion of his spirit.
[^463]: In Church Slavonic "а҆пфѡ́", transliterating the Greek Septuagint Ἀφφώ (4 Kings 2:14 LXX), representing the Hebrew אַף־הוּא (*'aph hu'*, "even He" / "where is He?").
[^464]: "коро́лю на́шемꙋ" — An explicit Ruthenian-Galician liturgical witness praying for the sovereign monarch of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth (King Augustus III, r. 1734–1763), reflecting the political and ecclesiastical context of the Pochaiv Lavra in 1761.
[^465]: Allusion to the judicial murder of Naboth orchestrated by Jezebel under cover of a proclaimed public fast (3 Kings 20 LXX / 1 Kings 21 Masoretic).
[^466]: Aposticha verse: Psalm 98:6 LXX (99:6).
[^467]: Sticheron draws on the prophetic lamentations of Jeremiah 12:1 and Habakkuk 1:2–3.
[^468]: Aposticha verse: Psalm 109:4 LXX (110:4).
[^469]: Matins Prokeimenon: Psalm 109:4 LXX (110:4).
[^470]: Prokeimenon verse: Psalm 109:1 LXX (110:1).
[^471]: Matins Gospel pericope: Luke 4:22–30 (Pericope 14).
[^472]: Acrostic in Church Slavonic: "Пою̀ хвалꙋ̀ вели́комꙋ и҆лїѝ" (I sing praise unto great Elijah). This canon was composed by the prolific 15th-century Athonite-Slavic hymnographer Pachomius the Serb (Pachomius Logothete).
[^473]: Slavonic "бг҃ода́рїаломъ" (plectrum, Greek πλήκτρῳ), an allegorical metaphor for divine inspiration.
[^474]: Melodic model: *Tone 8, Podoben: "The Wisdom" (Премꙋ́дрости)*, referring to the canonical model *Премꙋ́дрости и҆ ра́зꙋма* ("Of Wisdom and Prudence").
[^475]: Queen Jezebel (3 Kings / 1 Kings 19:1–3), who threatened Elijah's life after the slaughter of the prophets of Baal.
[^476]: The Transfiguration of Christ upon Mount Tabor (Matthew 17:1–8; Mark 9:2–8; Luke 9:28–36), where Elijah and Moses appeared in glory conversing with the Lord.
[^477]: The forty-day journey of Elijah through the wilderness unto Mount Horeb on the strength of the miraculous hearth cake and cruse of water provided by the angel of the Lord (3 Kings / 1 Kings 19:5–8).
[^478]: The revelation of God unto Elijah at the cave on Mount Horeb, not in the great and strong wind, nor in the earthquake, nor in the fire, but in the still small voice / gentle breeze (3 Kings / 1 Kings 19:11–12).
[^479]: The two captains of fifty and their men sent by King Ahaziah to seize Elijah, whom the prophet consumed with fire called down from heaven (4 Kings / 2 Kings 1:9–12).
[^480]: The contest on Mount Carmel against the four hundred and fifty prophets of Baal, followed by their slaughter at the brook Kishon (3 Kings / 1 Kings 18:20–40).
[^481]: The false prophets who ate at Queen Jezebel's royal table (3 Kings / 1 Kings 18:19).
[^482]: The divine judgment pronounced by Elijah against King Ahab and Queen Jezebel for the murder of Naboth the Jezreelite and the usurpation of his vineyard (3 Kings / 1 Kings 21:17–24).
[^483]: The two bands of fifty men under their captains consumed by fire from heaven at the word of Elijah (4 Kings / 2 Kings 1:9–12).
[^484]: Elijah parting the waters of the Jordan with his rolled-up mantle (4 Kings / 2 Kings 2:8).
[^485]: The assumption of Elijah into heaven in a fiery chariot and the transmission of his mantle to his disciple Elisha (4 Kings / 2 Kings 2:11–13).
[^486]: An apocryphal / patristic aggada regarding Elijah's unyielding zeal against sinners, wherein God translates the prophet to heaven so that the Lord Himself may show long-suffering and mercy toward Israel.
[^487]: Elijah raising the widow's dead son at Zarephath of Sidon (3 Kings / 1 Kings 17:17–24).
[^488]: King Augustus III of Poland (reigned 1734–1763), Grand Duke of Lithuania, under whose Polish-Lithuanian royal domain the Basilian monastery of Pochaiv printed this Menaion in 1761.
[^489]: St. Simeon of Emesa, fool for Christ's sake, and his fellow ascetic St. John the Silent (6th century), who spent many years in the desert near the Dead Sea.
[^490]: The mystical chariot of the Cherubim with wheels of fire full of eyes, the firmament as crystal, and the lightning-like flash of the divine glory revealed unto the Prophet Ezekiel by the river Chebar (Ezekiel 1:4–28; 10:1–22).
[^491]: Ezekiel commanded by God to lie upon his left side for 390 days to bear the iniquity of the house of Israel, and upon his right side for 40 days to bear the iniquity of the house of Judah (Ezekiel 4:4–8).
[^492]: The roll of a book given unto Ezekiel by the hand of the Lord, written within and without with lamentations and mourning and woe, which tasted as honey for sweetness in the prophet's mouth (Ezekiel 2:9–3:3).
[^493]: The Common Dismissal Troparion for Holy Monastics and Ascetic Fathers (Tone 4): "O God of our fathers, Who dealest ever with us according to Thy meekness..."
[^494]: The east gate of the sanctuary shown unto Ezekiel: "This gate shall be shut, it shall not be opened, and no man shall enter in by it; because the Lord, the God of Israel, hath entered in by it, therefore it shall be shut" (Ezekiel 44:1–3), universally interpreted in patristic and liturgical tradition as a type of the perpetual virginity (*semper virgo*) of the Theotokos.
[^495]: The prophetic vision of the valley filled with dry bones, which at the word of the Lord came together bone to bone, were clothed with sinews and flesh, and were brought to life by the breath of the Spirit (Ezekiel 37:1–14), prefiguring the general resurrection.
[^496]: The vision of the likeness of the throne of sapphire stone above the firmament over the heads of the Cherubim, and upon the likeness of the throne the likeness as the appearance of a man (Ezekiel 1:26–28; 10:1).
[^497]: St. Nikon, the hegumen of the monastery on Mount Sinai / the Jordan wilderness where St. Simeon the Fool for Christ and St. John the Silent were tonsured into the angelic monastic habit before entering upon their solitary desert struggles.
[^498]: The repetitive prophetic formula of Ezekiel: "The hand of the Lord was upon me" (Ezekiel 1:3; 3:14, 22; 8:1; 33:22; 37:1; 40:1).
[^499]: Slavonic witness: *Во все́мъ подо́бился є҆сѝ гдⷭ҇ни проро́къ: ѡ҆ чꙋжди́хъ бо согрѣше́нїихъ моля́шеся*. The text references the Prophet Ezekiel interceding for the sins of the people and prefiguring Christ.
[^500]: An allusion to the *Life of St. Simeon of Emesa, the Fool for Christ*, who entered the women's baths naked to demonstrate complete mortification of the passions and divine dispassion (*apatheia*).
[^501]: Cf. Ezekiel 1:26–28, wherein the prophet beholds the likeness of the throne of God borne above the Cherubim.
[^502]: Allusion to St. Simeon receiving sustenance and distributing food to the needy and outcasts of Emesa.
[^503]: The doctrines of Origen regarding the pre-existence of souls and universal restoration (*apokatastasis*) were condemned at the Fifth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople II, 553). The hymnographer praises St. Simeon for guarding his disciples against Origenist errors.
[^504]: An allusion to Ezekiel 40–48, describing the detailed visionary measuring of the new Temple and city of God.
[^505]: Cf. Ezekiel 8:3: "And He put forth the form of a hand, and took me by a lock of my head; and the spirit lifted me up between the earth and the heaven, and brought me in the visions of God to Jerusalem."
[^506]: Slavonic: *въ скро́вѣ мꙋ́дрыхъ чꙋде́съ пѣ́пене*, from the Greek πέπλος (*peplos*, veil or mantle), referring to the saint's feigned madness concealing his wonderworking grace.
[^507]: In the *Life of St. Simeon*, when his body was carried out for burial by poor citizens without clerical attendance, angelic choirs were heard chanting psalms and praises from heaven.
[^508]: Slavonic witness: *и҆здале́ча зри́ши во ѻ҆ц҃ѣ сн҃а и҆ дх҃а, трѝ ѵ҆поста́си бж҃тва*.
[^509]: The feast of July 22 unites the memory of the Holy Myrrh-Bearer and Equal-to-the-Apostles Mary Magdalene with the celebration of the return (translation) of the relics of the Hieromartyr Phocas, Bishop of Sinope, from the Black Sea coast to Constantinople under the reign of Arcadius and the patriarchate of St. John Chrysostom in 403/404.
[^510]: St. Phocas of Sinope was renowned throughout antiquity as the patron and protector of Christian mariners navigating tempestuous seas.
[^511]: The idiomelon of Anatolius at *Glory* reflects the patristic tradition (*Apostola Apostolorum*) of Mary Magdalene being the first person to encounter the risen Lord (Mark 16:9; John 20:11–18).
[^512]: John 20:14–17; the liturgical text poetically depicts the Lord appearing in the guise of the gardener (*вертогра́да храни́тель*) and washing Mary's sorrowful faith with the dew of His words (*росо́ю твои́хъ гла́съ, вѣ́рꙋ ѡ҆мы́въ*).
[^513]: An allusion to Luke 8:2 and Mark 16:9, recording that seven demons were cast out of Mary Magdalene by the Lord.
[^514]: Allusion to a prominent wonder in the *Life of St. Phocas of Sinope*, wherein an impure spirit tormenting a ship's crew fled upon the bishop's prayer.
[^515]: The refrain of the Kontakion (*прїидѝ фѡ́ко съ на́ми, и҆ никто́же на ны̀*) mirrors the historic nautical custom of mariners in the Black Sea and the Levant invoking St. Phocas during hazardous voyages.
[^516]: Cf. Luke 1:48: "For, behold, from henceforth all generations shall call me blessed."
[^517]: In the *Life of the Hieromartyr Phocas*, during his interrogation before the pagan magistrate, a white dove from heaven descended and rested upon his head, presaging his crown of martyrdom.
[^518]: Eve (the foremother) is depicted rejoicing at the courage of the Myrrh-Bearing women, who by their steadfast faith and manly spirit (*нра́въ мꙋ́жескій*) trampled underfoot the serpent who had seduced mankind in Eden.
[^519]: The hymnographer refers to the preaching of St. Mary Magdalene following Christ's Ascension, where according to ancient tradition she traveled to Rome and proclaimed the Resurrection of Christ.
[^520]: Cf. John 20:15, where Mary Magdalene, seeing the risen Christ, "supposed him to be the gardener" (*вертогра́даря того̀ непщева́ла є҆сѝ*).
[^521]: Cf. Luke 1:68, 69, 78, 79 (The Canticle of Zechariah / Benedictus).
[^522]: Cf. Dan. 2:34–35, 44–45.
[^523]: John 20:17.
[^524]: Commemoration of the holy martyrs Trophimus, Theophilus, and thirteen other martyrs who suffered under Diocletian (c. 304).
[^525]: Attributed to St. Andrew, Archbishop of Crete (c. 660–740).
[^526]: The hymnographic context here relates to the Dormition / translation (*премѣне́нїе*) announced to the Virgin, or the festal glad tidings.
[^527]: Allusion to the conversion of the pagan executioner or persecutor (Theophilus) through the fortitude and preaching of Trophimus.
[^528]: Cf. Habakkuk 3:2–3 (LXX).
[^529]: Play on the Greek name *Theophilus* (Θεόφιλος, "lover of God" / "God-beloved").
[^530]: Cf. Jonah 2:2–7.
[^531]: The phrase "shining threefold fourfold" (*четвери́цею тро́йственно сїя́ѧ*) references the company of twelve plus three (fifteen total) martyrs.
[^532]: Cf. Exodus 3:2.
[^533]: Commemoration of the Holy Great-Martyr Christina of Tyre (c. 300); and the Holy Passion-Bearers Boris and Gleb, sons of Grand Prince Volodymyr the Great, baptized as Roman and David, slain in 1015.
[^534]: Cf. Romans 8:35, 38–39.
[^535]: The Church Slavonic acrostic reflects Joseph the Hymnographer's Greek verse acrostic: *Χριστοῦ σε μέλπω παρθένον φερώνυμον* ("I sing of thee, the maiden named after Christ").
[^536]: The Pochaiv Menaion rubric designates Tone 4 (`гла́съ, д҃`), yet assigns the podoben *Гро́бъ тво́й* (*Thy Tomb, O Savior*), which in the standard Byzantine-Slavonic melodic system belongs to Tone 1.
[^537]: The Church Slavonic text employs the Ruthenian-Galician liturgical borrowing *пѣнзеловавщи* (from Polish *pędzlować* / Middle High German *pensel*, to paint or depict with a fine artist's brush), referring to the hagiographical account of the Great-Martyr Christina depicting or fashioning three windows or the sign of the Holy Trinity in her chamber.
[^538]: Church Slavonic *де́кла* is the Western Ruthenian/Slavonic transcription of the Greek name *Θέκλα* (*Thecla*, Protomartyr and Equal-to-the-Apostles).
[^539]: The Pochaiv Menaion abbreviates the automelon model heading to *Ра́дꙋйсѧ*, indicating the standard Tone 5 model *Ра́дꙋйся живоно́сный Крⷭ҇те* (*Rejoice, O life-bearing Cross*).
[^540]: The prescribed Epistle lection for the Holy Great-Martyr Christina is designated as pericope 181 (`за, р҃па`), comprising 2 Corinthians 6:1–10.
[^541]: The Gospel lection for the Great-Martyr Christina is Luke pericope 33 (`зача́ло, л҃г`), comprising Luke 7:36–50.
[^542]: The Holy Passion-Bearers Princes Boris and Gleb were baptized with the Christian names Romanus and David respectively, under which names they are primarily commemorated in the Byzantine-Slavonic hymnography.
[^543]: Church Slavonic *рѡссі́йскимъ страна́мъ* designates the historical territorial and ecclesiastical lands of Rus / Ruthenia.
[^544]: The Pochaiv Menaion rubric leaves the tone designation blank after *Сла́ва, гла́съ*; the musical and textual tradition assigns this doxasticon to Tone 2.
[^545]: Church Slavonic *сыновѡ́мъ рѡссі́йскимъ* (unto the sons of Rus / Ruthenia).
[^546]: Wisdom of Solomon 4:7–15 (Septuagint versification). The third festal Old Testament lection concludes at verse 15 on folio 125r (`рк҃є`).
[^547]: Church Slavonic *рѡссі́йстїи собо́ри* (Ruthenian / Rus' assemblies), designating the Christian flock and churches of Kyivan Rus'.
[^548]: Cross-reference to the Troparion of the Holy Passion-Bearers Boris and Gleb printed at Little Vespers on fol. 124r (`Page_0249.jpg`).
[^549]: The Matins Gospel lection for the Holy Martyrs Boris and Gleb is Luke pericope 106 (`зача́ло, р҃ѕ`), comprising Luke 21:12–19.
[^550]: The acrostic of the canon to the Birthgiver of God across Odes 1–9 forms the phrase: *Пою̀ хвалꙋ̀ живоно́снымъ ѻ҆трокови́цы* (I sing praise to the life-bearing Maiden).
[^551]: The standard festal Katavasia for Odes 1–8 is the Canon of the Dormition / Theotokos: *I shall open my mouth* (`Ѿве́рзꙋ оу҆ста̀ моя̀`).
[^552]: Ode 4 Heirmos draws typologically from the canticle of the Prophet Habakkuk (Habakkuk 3:2 LXX).
[^553]: A theological affirmation of the Chalcedonian dogma of the two natures of Christ against Docetism: *а҆ не мечта́нїемъ* (and not in phantom/apparition).
[^554]: Church Slavonic *пога́ныхъ оу҆ставля́й шата̑нїя* (put an end to the insolence of the pagans / infidels), a standard Byzantine-Slavonic hymnographic supplication for peace and deliverance from invading pagan hordes (Pechenegs, Polovtsians, Tatars).
[^555]: Church Slavonic *и҆ми́рно строе́нїе дарова́ти королю̀ на́шемꙋ* (and grant peaceful governance unto our King). A distinct monarchical petition of the Pochaiv Lavra edition, referring to the reigning monarch of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth (King Augustus III, r. 1734–1763).
[^556]: Church Slavonic *во все́й землѝ россі́йстѣй* (in all the land of Rus' / Ruthenian land), identifying Prince Roman (Boris) as ruler in Rostov and David (Gleb) in Murom within the realm of St. Vladimir of Kyiv.
[^557]: Typological citation from Daniel 6:22 and Daniel 3 / Song of the Three Holy Children (LXX).
[^558]: An account from the *Narrative and Passion and Encomium of the Holy Martyrs Boris and Gleb* (*Сказание и страсть и похвала святую мученику Бориса и Глеба*): following the murder of Prince Gleb (David) on the Smiadyn River near Smolensk in 1015, his body was left unburied in a desolate place between logs, where passing hunters later observed a miraculous column of fire and heard angels singing over his incorrupt relics.
[^559]: Song of Songs 5:16: "His mouth is most sweet, yea, He is altogether lovely."
[^560]: Daniel 2:34, 45: the stone cut out of the mountain without hands as an ancient typological prophecy of the Virgin Birth of Christ.
[^561]: An allusion to the hagiographical account of miracles at the shrine of Saints Boris and Gleb in Vyshhorod, where an irreverent intruder / thief attempting to violate the reliquary was cast down by a terrifying apparition of fire.
[^562]: The Exapostilarion model *Посѣти́лъ ны́ е҆́стъ* ("Our Savior from on high hath visited us") is based on the Dayspring canticle of Zechariah (Luke 1:78).
[^563]: Melodic model *Небе́сныхъ чинѡ́въ ра́дованїе* ("Joy of the heavenly hosts"), Tone 1.
[^564]: The Liturgy readings for Saints Boris and Gleb: Prokeimenon Psalm 15:3; Epistle pericope 99 (Romans 8:28–39); Alleluia Psalm 33:18; Gospel pericope 52 (John 15:17–16:2); Communion verse Psalm 32:1.
[^565]: Melodic model *Да́лъ є҆сѝ зна́менїе* ("Thou hast given a sign"), Tone 4.
[^566]: Melodic model *Ѡ҆ ди́внагѡ чудесѐ* ("O marvelous wonder"), Tone 4.
[^567]: Hymnographic composition attributed to Anatolius, Patriarch of Constantinople (d. 458) or Anatolius the Studite.
[^568]: The three Old Testament lections appointed for the Dormition of St. Anna are from the Wisdom of Solomon: Wisdom 5:15–6:3, Wisdom 3:1–9, and Wisdom 4:7–15 (LXX), cross-referenced to the feast of St. Athanasius of Athos on July 5 (fol. 28r).
[^569]: Melodic model *Ра́дуйсѧ, по́стникѡмъ* ("Rejoice, [thou guide] of the ascetics"), Tone 5.
[^570]: Festal Troparion of the Dormition of St. Anna: *Жи́знь ро́ждшую во чре́вѣ носи́ла є҆сѝ* ("Thou didst bear in thy womb her who bore Life").
[^571]: The alphabetical acrostic of the canon to the Holy Women Olympias and Euphraxia across Odes 1–9 forms the Greek phrase: *Честны́мъ жена́мъ чⷭ҇тно́е пою̀ пѣ́нїе. І҆ѡ́сифъ* ("I sing an honorable hymn to the honorable women. [By] Joseph", i.e., St. Joseph the Hymnographer).
[^572]: The prophetic symbols of the Theotokos: the gate of light (Ezekiel 44:2) and the living scroll inscribed not by human hand (Isaiah 8:1; Psalm 44:2 LXX).
[^573]: Compline Ode 5 troparion: "the sacred shepherd who watered the universe with all-golden teachings" explicitly references Saint John Chrysostom, Archbishop of Constantinople, who spiritually directed and consoled Saint Olympias during his exiles.
[^574]: Melodic model *Повелѣ́нное та́йнѡ* ("The mystery commanded in secret"), Tone 8.
[^575]: Church Slavonic *въ патрїа́рсѣхъ вели́кїй златосло́въ* ("the great Golden-mouthed among the Patriarchs"), identifying Saint John Chrysostom by his canonical title.
[^576]: Tone 1 automelon *Небе́сныхъ чинѡ́въ* (Joy of the heavenly hosts / Of the celestial ranks), corresponding to Byzantine Greek *Τῶν οὐρανίων ταγμάτων*.
[^577]: The printed Slavonic reads masculine accusative *преста́вльшагося того́* ('Him Who was translated') where liturgical syntax expects feminine nominative *преста́вльшаяся* ('thou who hast been translated').
[^578]: The printed witness *моля́щи сн҃а твоего̀ съ ма́терїю свое́ю* ('praying to Thy Son with His Mother') reflects an elevated Ruthenian hymnic trope coordinating intercessory supplication before the Savior alongside His all-pure Mother.
[^579]: Gospel pericope: Matthew 56 (Matt. 13:44–54 LXX/Slavonic), appointed for righteous women and monastics.
[^580]: Tone 8 automelon *Премꙋ́дрости* (The Wisdom and Word / By the Wisdom and Word), corresponding to Byzantine Greek *Τὴν Σοφίαν καὶ Λόγον*.
[^581]: Tone 2 automelon *Вы́шнихъ и҆щѧ̀* (Seeking the highest / Seeking the things on high), corresponding to Byzantine Greek *Τὰ ἄνω ζητῶν*.
[^582]: Codicological crux: Physical leaf p276 is a duplicate photographic exposure of Fol. 137r (p275) present in the digitized facsimile archive, preserving the identical printed text of the Ikos and Odes 7–8.
[^583]: Tone 2 automelon *Со оу҆чени́ки* (With the disciples / When the disciples), corresponding to Byzantine Greek *Τοῖς μαθηταῖς συνέλθωμεν*.
[^584]: Automelon *Не́бо ѕвѣзда́ми* (Heaven with stars / O Thou Who hast adorned heaven with stars), corresponding to Byzantine Greek *Ὁ οὐρανὸν τοῖς ἄστροις*.
[^585]: Epistle pericope: Galatians 210 (Gal. 4:22–31), setting forth the allegorical distinction between the bondwoman Hagar and the freewoman Sarah, appointed for feasts of righteous ancestral mothers.
[^586]: Gospel pericope: Luke 36 (Lk. 8:16–21), on the candle set upon a lampstand and Christ's mother and brethren.
[^587]: Feast of the Holy Hieromartyr Hermolaus and his companions Hermippus and Hermocrates, presbyters of Nicomedia martyred under Maximian (c. 305).
[^588]: Tone 8 automelon *Ѡ҆ пресла́внагѡ чꙋдесѐ* (O most marvelous wonder / O most glorious marvel), corresponding to Byzantine Greek *Ὢ τοῦ παραδόξου θαύματος*.
[^589]: The acrostic of the canon by St. Joseph the Hymnographer reads in Church Slavonic: *Пѣ́сньми почита́ю є҆рмола́а трꙋды̀. І҆ѡ́сифъ* ('With songs I honor the labors of Hermolaus. Joseph').
[^590]: Tone 8 automelon *Премꙋ́дрости* (The Wisdom and Word / By the Wisdom and Word), corresponding to Byzantine Greek *Τὴν Σοφίαν καὶ Λόγον*.
[^591]: St. Hermolaus, priest of Nicomedia, along with his fellow presbyters Hermippus and Hermocrates, survived the burning of the 20,000 martyrs of Nicomedia in 303, later catechized and baptized St. Panteleimon, and suffered beheading c. 305 under the emperor Maximian.
[^592]: Dan. 3:19–28, the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace, the Scriptural canticle prototype for Odes 7 and 8 of the Byzantine hymnographic canon.
[^593]: The holy martyr was originally named Pantoleon ("all-lion"), but upon his baptism and martyric contest was named Panteleimon ("all-merciful", Greek: Παντελεήμων) in token of the compassion and gratuitous healing he bestowed on all who approached him.
[^594]: The printed foliation leaps directly from Fol. 138v (`рл҃и`, physical leaf p283) to Fol. 141r (`рм҃а`, physical leaf p284), omitting numbers 139 and 140. The catchword `ꙗ҆вль́ся` at the bottom of p283 matches the opening text of p284 (`ꙗ҆́влься хрⷭта̀ бг҃а на́шегѡ`), demonstrating unbroken textual continuity with zero missing leaves.
[^595]: The three Old Testament readings for Great-Martyrs are printed in full under July 24 (St. Christina), fol. 124r (physical leaf p257): Isaiah 43:9–14 ("Thus says the Lord: All the nations..."), Wisdom of Solomon 3:1–9 ("The souls of the righteous are in the hand of God..."), and Wisdom of Solomon 4:7–15 ("Though the righteous happen to die early...").
[^596]: A reference to the miracle recounted in the Life of St. Panteleimon: encountering a child dead from the bite of an echidna (viper), the saint prayed to Christ that the child be resurrected and the serpent burst asunder, which miracle brought him immediately to Holy Baptism by St. Hermolaus.
[^597]: Slavonic `ма́рдїю` (witnessing Byzantine Greek μάρμαρον or an epigraphic rendering of spiritual endurance/luster): referring to the steadfast, unyielding character of the martyr reflecting divine compassion and steadfast patience.
[^598]: Gospel of Luke, pericope 106 (Luke 21:12–19), the festal Matins Gospel appointed for great martyrs ("The Lord said to His disciples: Beware of men; for they will lay their hands on you and persecute you...").
[^599]: The canon is the work of St. Theophanes the Branded (Graptos, c. 778–845); its acrostic in Greek forms Παντελεήμονα παναρίδηλον μέлπω, rendered faithfully in the Church Slavonic text as `Пантелеи́мона всеизря́днаго мꙋ́ченика пою̀` ("I sing of Panteleimon the most excellent martyr").
[^600]: An allusion to the elder priest Hermolaus who instructed Panteleimon in spiritual wisdom and baptized him, framed typologically against the righteous elder Simeon who took Christ into his arms in the Temple (Luke 2:25–35).
[^601]: An allusion to Psalm 65:12 (LXX): "We went through fire and through water, but Thou broughtest us out into a place of refreshment."
[^602]: Dan. 3:24–28; the canticle of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace, where the angel of the Lord came down and made the midst of the furnace like a bedewing breeze.
[^603]: Gen. 3:24; the flaming sword turning every way to guard the way of the tree of life, turned aside and reopened to believers through the blood flowing from Christ's pierced side (John 19:34).
[^604]: Slavonic `Ѡнти́нское` (witnessing Greek ὀνθώδη or an epigraphic rendering of corrupt philosophical chatter): contrasting empty medical speculation with the grace-filled healing wrought through the invocation of Christ's name.
[^605]: The catchword `дѣ́ломъ` ("in deed") at the foot of fol. 144v matches the Greek text of Ode 9 (ἔργῳ), though omitted at the start of fol. 145r by the typesetter.
[^606]: Dan. 6:16–23; the Prophet Daniel in the lions' den, a recurring typological model for Christian martyrs unharmed by ravenous beasts in the arena.
[^607]: Etymological wordplay on the martyr's name *Panteleimon* (Greek: Παντελεήμων, "all-merciful"), compound of πᾶς ("all") and ἐлеήμων ("merciful").
[^608]: Pericope 292 of the Apostle (2 Tim. 2:1–10: "My son Timothy, be strong in the grace that is in Christ Jesus...").
[^609]: Pericope 52 of the Gospel according to John (John 15:17–16:2: "The Lord said to His disciples: These things I command you, that ye love one another...").
[^610]: Acts 6:1–6; the appointment and ordination of the first seven deacons in Jerusalem to oversee the daily distribution and serve tables (Prochorus, Nicanor, Timon, and Parmenas are commemorated together on July 28).
[^611]: St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), one of the prolific Melodists of the Studite tradition, whose poetic acrostic is embedded in Church Slavonic as `Пою̀ зарѝ мы́сленнагѡ сл҃нца. Їѡ́сифъ` ("I sing of the rays of the noetic Sun. Joseph").
[^612]: St. Prochorus, disciple of the Apostle Peter and companion of St. John the Theologian on Patmos, was consecrated first bishop of Nicomedia in Bithynia, where he faithfully shepherded the flock until his martyric death.
[^613]: Tradition records that St. Nicanor suffered martyrdom in Jerusalem on the very day of the stoning of St. Stephen the Protomartyr (Acts 7), being slain along with two thousand believers in the intense persecution described in Acts 8:1.
[^614]: St. Timon was appointed bishop of Bostra (Bozrah) in Arabia Petræa, where he was condemned by the pagans and cast into a furnace, receiving the crown of martyrdom.
[^615]: Unlike his three fellow deacons who met violent martyric deaths, St. Parmenas is recorded to have died peacefully in the Lord in Jerusalem while fulfilling his service at the tables of the poor, mourned and buried by the Apostles themselves.
[^616]: Luke 10:1; the Holy Seventy Apostles, among whose ranks the holy deacons Prochorus, Nicanor, Timon, and Parmenas are numbered alongside the primary Twelve.
[^617]: St. Callinicus of Gangra in Paphlagonia, who preached the Gospel throughout Asia Minor, was arrested under the governor Sacerdon, shod with iron boots lined with sharp nails, forced to run to Gangra, and cast into a blazing furnace c. 250 (or c. 304), wherein he yielded his soul while his body remained entirely untouched by the flame.
[^618]: Ex. 14:7 (LXX); τριστάτας (Slavonic: `трїста́ты`, three-man chariot officers of Pharaoh), here allegorized as the demonic principalities drowned in the martyr's outpouring of blood.
[^619]: Ps. 118:14 (LXX): "I have rejoiced in the way of Thy testimonies, as in all riches." In the Acts of the saint, when his feet were pierced by iron nails within his shoes, he chanted this psalm verse as he marched unto the place of his martyrdom.
[^620]: The Pochaiv typesetter prints `поже́рши` (as if from `пожре́ти`, "to sacrifice/offer"), an epigraphic corruption or variant for `ро́ждши` ("having brought forth/given birth to"), which is the standard reading of this Menaion Theotokion.
[^621]: Dan. 3:19–27; the canticle of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace quenched by the angel of the Lord, prefiguring Callinicus surviving the fire.
[^622]: St. Callinicus was condemned by the governor Sacerdon to be shod in iron boots lined with sharp nails and forced to run sixty miles to Gangra, where he was martyred in a furnace.
[^623]: Typological Marian figures: the tabernacle (Ex. 40), the table of showbread (Ex. 25:23), the jar of manna (Heb. 9:4), the golden lampstand (Ex. 25:31), the holy mountain (Dan. 2:34), the cloud (Isa. 19:1), the stone tablets of the law (Ex. 31:18), the ark of the covenant (Ex. 25:10), Aaron's budding rod (Num. 17:8), and the shut eastern gate (Ezek. 44:1–3).
[^624]: The Holy Apostles of the Seventy commemorated together on July 30: Silas (Acts 15:22, companion of Paul), Silvanus (1 Pet. 5:12, 2 Cor. 1:19), Crescens (2 Tim. 4:10, bishop of Chalcedon or Galatia), Epenetus (Rom. 16:5, bishop of Carthage), and Andronicus (Rom. 16:7, bishop of Pannonia).
[^625]: Ex. 14:21–29; the miraculous passage of the Israelites dryshod through the Red Sea under Moses.
[^626]: 1 Sam. 2:1–2 (LXX: 1 Kings 2:1–2); the Canticle of Hannah ("My heart is established in the Lord... there is none holy as the Lord, neither is there any righteous like our God").
[^627]: Hab. 3:2 (LXX); the Canticle of the Prophet Habakkuk ("O Lord, I have heard Thy report and was afraid; I considered Thy works and was astonished").
[^628]: Ps. 67:16–17 (LXX); "The mountain of God is a fertile mountain, a curdled mountain... the mountain wherein God is pleased to dwell," understood typologically of the Virgin Theotokos.
[^629]: Jonah 2:2–9; the prayer of the Prophet Jonah from the belly of the sea-monster, a type of Christ's descent into hades and resurrection.
[^630]: Dan. 3:57–88; the canticle of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace ("Bless the Lord, all ye works of the Lord; sing and exalt Him above all forever").
[^631]: The Forefeast of the Procession of the Honorable and Life-Creating Cross of the Lord, commemorating the annual Byzantine custom of bringing the Precious Wood from the Imperial palace chapel in Constantinople through the streets of the city to avert seasonal summer illnesses in August.
[^632]: St. Eudocimus the Righteous of Cappadocia (c. 800–840), military governor (tourmarch) of the Charsianon theme under Emperor Theophilus, celebrated for his secret asceticism, strict chastity, and boundless charity to the needy; his relics remained completely incorrupt after burial.
[^633]: Ps. 98:5 (LXX); "Exalt ye the Lord our God, and worship at the footstool of His feet, for He is holy."
[^634]: Historical Ruthenian-Galician liturgical witness: the Pochaiv print preserves the royal petition for the reigning Polish-Lithuanian monarch (`бл҃говѣ́рномꙋ коро́лю на́шемꙋ`, "to our pious King", designating King Augustus III of Poland, reigning 1733–1763), where contemporary Muscovite editions read `бл҃говѣ́рномꙋ ї҆мпера́торꙋ на́шемꙋ` or `царю̀`.
[^635]: Ex. 15:1–19; the Triumph Song of Moses after the drowning of Pharaoh's host in the Red Sea.
[^636]: 1 Sam. 2:2 (LXX: 1 Kings 2:2); the Canticle of Hannah ("There is none holy as the Lord, neither is there any righteous like our God").
[^637]: St. Eudocimus' name in Greek signifies "esteemed, honored, of good reputation" (from εὖ, "well" + δοκέω, "to think/seem"); the hymnographer makes repeated wordplay on this etymology (`благоволивъ благими дѣянїи`).
[^638]: Hab. 3:2 (LXX); Canticle of Habakkuk ("In the midst of two living creatures Thou shalt be known; when the years draw near Thou shalt be recognized").
[^639]: Ex. 17:6, Num. 20:11; the rock struck by Moses in the wilderness that poured forth water, understood typologically of the Life-Creating Cross of Christ.
[^640]: St. Eudocimus was buried in Charsianon; eighteen months later his mother opened the tomb and found his body completely incorrupt, fragrant, and performing miraculous healings; his relics were later translated to the Church of the Theotokos in Constantinople.
[^641]: A direct play on the saint's name Eudocimus (`тезоименитно`, "consistently with thy name", εὐ-δόкиμος).
[^642]: Jonah 2:2–9; the prayer of the Prophet Jonah from the belly of the sea-monster.
[^643]: Ex. 14:21–29; Moses prefiguring the power of the Cross by dividing the Red Sea with his staff.
[^644]: Dan. 3:49–50; the angel of the Lord descending into the furnace and driving away the flame with a moist whistling wind (spiritual dew).
[^645]: Dan. 3:57; the Benedicite canticle of the Three Children in Babylon ("O all ye works of the Lord, bless ye the Lord").
[^646]: Constantine the Great's vision of the luminous Sign of the Cross in the heavens before the Battle of the Milvian Bridge (312 AD) with the inscription *In hoc vince* ("By this conquer").
[^647]: Ps. 98:5 (LXX); "Exalt ye the Lord our God, and worship at the footstool of His feet, for He is holy."
[^648]: Dan. 3:25; the fourth figure in the furnace resembling the Son of God, prefiguring the Virgin Birth.
[^649]: Ps. 68:15 (LXX); "Deliver me out of the mire, that I may not stick fast: let me be delivered from them that hate me, and out of the deep waters."
[^650]: Ps. 71:6 (LXX), Judg. 6:37–40; the descent of Christ into the Virgin's womb typified by the dew descending upon Gideon's fleece.
[^651]: Matt. 25:1–13; the parable of the Wise Virgins entering the bright bridal chamber with lit lamps.
[^652]: 1 Thess. 5:5; "Ye are all the children of light, and the children of the day: we are not of the night, nor of darkness."
[^653]: Ps. 73:12 (LXX); "Yet God is our King before the ages: He hath wrought salvation in the midst of the earth."
[^654]: Ps. 115:6 (LXX); "Precious in the sight of the Lord is the death of His venerable ones."
[^655]: Gal. 5:22–6:2 (Pericope 213); the liturgical epistle pericope appointed for venerable monks and righteous men ("The fruit of the Spirit is love, joy, peace...").
[^656]: The coronation of the Miraculous Icon of the Mother of God of Pochaiv took place on September 8, 1773, by decree of Pope Clement XIV. Although this print bears the imprint date of 1761, the Pochaiv Lavra printshop inserted this celebratory copperplate engraving into remaining unbound quires after the 1773 papal coronation.
